I mean, I feel like I'm looking right at the tape measure or the paper when I'm doing it. But... I don't know. Maybe my mind is just wandering for a split second and I don't realize it. It's hard to say for sure. I feel like I'm trying to pay attention, but the exact details just somehow slip past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you understand the instructions or information correctly when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I understand things just fine. I mean, I know exactly what I'm supposed to do. If I read a set of instructions or look at the schedule, it all makes perfect sense to me. \n\nIt's just... when it comes time to actually execute it, that's when the mistake happens. Like with the wood, I know exactly how long it needs to be, I just end up marking it wrong anyway. So, no, I don't think I'm misunderstanding the information at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're processing or taking in the information correctly when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean... it feels like I'm taking it in just fine. I look at the numbers or the paper, and they register in my head okay. So I think I'm processing it right. \n\nBut... I don't know, maybe I'm not? It's hard to tell exactly what's happening in my brain at that exact second. I just know that I read it, I understand it, and then somewhere between my head and my hands, something just gets crossed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are happening because you're not focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is a focus thing, yeah. I mean, that's really what brings me in to see you. I just keep messing up these little things. Like, I'll be looking right at the schedule for the senior center, and I'll end up writing down the wrong time for a class. Or with the boards I mentioned... I'll measure it once, but I still cut it a hair short because I just sort of skipped over the small print on the tape measure. So, yeah, I guess I'm just not focusing enough on those tiny details when I'm in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're noticing all the details when you're performing a task?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I guess I'm not noticing all of them. Like I was just saying, that's really why I'm here. I keep messing up those little things. I'll be looking right at the senior center schedule and write down the wrong time for a class. Or with the woodworking, I'll measure a board, but I still cut it a hair short because I just... skip over the small print on the tape measure. It's nothing huge, but... well, for example, I'll write down the wrong time on a class sheet down at the senior center. Or I'll be working on a project, measure a board once, and still cut it a hair short because I somehow skipped over the small print on the tape measure. I just keep making these little detail mistakes, and it's getting frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're forgetting things more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. My memory seems about the same as it always has been. I mean, I'm not forgetting appointments or misplacing things any more than usual. It's really just... well, making those little errors when I'm actually doing something, rather than forgetting things completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're paying attention when you make these little errors?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I am. I mean, I feel like I'm looking right at the tape measure or the paper when I'm doing it. But... I don't know. Maybe my mind is just wandering for a split second and I don't realize it. It's hard to say for sure. I feel like I'm trying to pay attention, but the exact details just somehow slip past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you understand the instructions or information correctly when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I understand things just fine. I mean, I know exactly what I'm supposed to do. If I read a set of instructions or look at the schedule, it all makes perfect sense to me. \n\nIt's just... when it comes time to actually execute it, that's when the mistake happens. Like with the wood, I know exactly how long it needs to be, I just end up marking it wrong anyway. So, no, I don't think I'm misunderstanding the information at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're processing or taking in the information correctly when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean... it feels like I'm taking it in just fine. I look at the numbers or the paper, and they register in my head okay. So I think I'm processing it right. \n\nBut... I don't know, maybe I'm not? It's hard to tell exactly what's happening in my brain at that exact second. I just know that I read it, I understand it, and then somewhere between my head and my hands, something just gets crossed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are happening because you're not focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is a focus thing, yeah. I mean, that's really what brings me in to see you. I just keep messing up these little things. Like, I'll be looking right at the schedule for the senior center, and I'll end up writing down the wrong time for a class. Or with the boards I mentioned... I'll measure it once, but I still cut it a hair short because I just sort of skipped over the small print on the tape measure. So, yeah, I guess I'm just not focusing enough on those tiny details when I'm in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're noticing all the details when you're performing a task?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I guess I'm not noticing all of them. Like I was just saying, that's really why I'm here. I keep messing up those little things. I'll be looking right at the senior center schedule and write down the wrong time for a class. Or with the woodworking, I'll measure a board, but I still cut it a hair short because I just... skip over the small print on the tape measure. So I guess I'm missing those little details when I'm actually doing the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're distracted by things around you when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At the senior center, if people are talking at the next table or... if my phone lights up, my mind just jumps over there. And then I lose my place in whatever I was filling out, or building, or whatever I was doing. So yeah, the things around me definitely pull me away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making these little errors in a variety of tasks, or is it mostly in one specific type of activity?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just one thing, I suppose. I mean, like I said, it happens with the woodworking out in the garage, and it happens with the scheduling stuff at the senior center. I don't really know if it happens in a whole lot of other areas, mostly because... well, since I stopped working, I don't have a ton of different activities going on anyway. But yeah, between my hobbies and the center, it's definitely not just one specific type of task. It just sort of happens with whatever I'm trying to get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're aware of your own thoughts when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess I'm not paying much attention to what I'm thinking about right at that second. My mind is just sort of... somewhere else. I couldn't tell you exactly what's going through my head when I write down the wrong time or make a bad cut. I just know I'm not thinking about the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to plan and organize tasks successfully before you start working on them?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I struggle with that quite a bit, actually. Like... if I have a day where I need to run a few errands, get the workshop ready for a project, and maybe go to a doctor's appointment, I have a really hard time figuring out what order to do them in. I just can't seem to get a plan straight in my head. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping to do something else, and then by the end of the day I'm just rushing around trying to finish it all. So, no, planning and organizing isn't something I do very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or daze when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say a fog, exactly. Or a daze. It's not like I'm out of it or confused. It's just... like I said before, my mind is just somewhere else. I'm just not focusing on what my hands are doing. So no, I don't feel foggy, just distracted, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So yeah, the things around me definitely pull me away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making these little errors in a variety of tasks, or is it mostly in one specific type of activity?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just one thing, I suppose. I mean, like I said, it happens with the woodworking out in the garage, and it happens with the scheduling stuff at the senior center. I don't really know if it happens in a whole lot of other areas, mostly because... well, since I stopped working, I don't have a ton of different activities going on anyway. But yeah, between my hobbies and the center, it's definitely not just one specific type of task. It just sort of happens with whatever I'm trying to get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're aware of your own thoughts when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess I'm not paying much attention to what I'm thinking about right at that second. My mind is just sort of... somewhere else. I couldn't tell you exactly what's going through my head when I write down the wrong time or make a bad cut. I just know I'm not thinking about the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to plan and organize tasks successfully before you start working on them?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I struggle with that quite a bit, actually. Like... if I have a day where I need to run a few errands, get the workshop ready for a project, and maybe go to a doctor's appointment, I have a really hard time figuring out what order to do them in. I just can't seem to get a plan straight in my head. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping to do something else, and then by the end of the day I'm just rushing around trying to finish it all. So, no, planning and organizing isn't something I do very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or daze when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say a fog, exactly. Or a daze. It's not like I'm out of it or confused. It's just... like I said before, my mind is just somewhere else. I'm just not focusing on what my hands are doing. So no, I don't feel foggy, just distracted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if you slowed down and took your time when performing tasks, you would still make these little errors?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't... I haven't really paid attention to whether how fast I'm going makes a difference. It's hard to say for sure. I just know that I read it, I understand it, and then somewhere between my head and my hands, something just gets crossed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are happening because you're not focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is a focus thing, yeah. I mean, I feel like I'm looking right at the tape measure or the paper when I'm doing it. But... I don't know. Maybe my mind is just wandering for a split second and I don't realize it. It's hard to say for sure. I feel like I'm trying to pay attention, but the exact details just somehow slip past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you understand the instructions or information correctly when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I understand things just fine. I mean, I know exactly what I'm supposed to do. If I read a set of instructions or look at the schedule, it all makes perfect sense to me. \n\nIt's just... when it comes time to actually execute it, that's when the mistake happens. Like with the wood, I know exactly how long it needs to be, I just end up marking it wrong anyway. So, no, I don't think I'm misunderstanding the information at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're processing or taking in the information correctly when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean... it feels like I'm taking it in just fine. I look at the numbers or the paper, and they register in my head okay. So I think I'm processing it right. \n\nBut... I don't know, maybe I'm not? It's hard to tell exactly what's happening in my brain at that exact second. I just know that I read it, I understand it, and then somewhere between my head and my hands, something just gets crossed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are happening because you're not focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is a focus thing, yeah. I mean, that's really what brings me in to see you. I just keep messing up these little things. Like, I'll be looking right at the schedule for the senior center, and I'll end up writing down the wrong time for a class. Or with the boards I mentioned... I'll measure it once, but I still cut it a hair short because I just sort of skipped over the small print on the tape measure. So, yeah, I guess I'm just not focusing enough on those tiny details when I'm in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're noticing all the details when you're performing a task?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I guess I'm not noticing all of them. Like I was just saying, that's really why I'm here. I keep messing up those little things. I'll be looking right at the senior center schedule and write down the wrong time for a class. Or with the woodworking, I'll measure a board, but I still cut it a hair short because I just... skip over the small print on the tape measure. So I guess I'm missing those little details when I'm actually doing the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're distracted by things around you when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At the senior center, if people are talking at the next table or... if my phone lights up, my mind just jumps over there. And then I lose my place in whatever I was filling out, or building, or whatever I was doing. I mean, like I said, it happens with the woodworking out in the garage, and it happens with the scheduling stuff at the senior center. I just can't seem to get a plan straight in my head. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping to do something else, and then by the end of the day I'm just rushing around trying to finish it all. So, no, planning and organizing isn't something I do very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or daze when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say a fog, exactly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF98.8, R40.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I just keep messing up little things. It's nothing huge, but... well, for example, I'll write down the wrong time on a class sheet down at the senior center. Or I'll be working on a project, measure a board once, and still cut it a hair short because I somehow skipped over the small print on the tape measure. I just keep making these little detail mistakes, and it's getting frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're forgetting things more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. My memory seems about the same as it always has been. I mean, I'm not forgetting appointments or misplacing things any more than usual. It's really just... well, making those little errors when I'm actually doing something, rather than forgetting things completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're paying attention when you make these little errors?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I am. I mean, I feel like I'm looking right at the tape measure or the paper when I'm doing it. But... I don't know. Maybe my mind is just wandering for a split second and I don't realize it. It's hard to say for sure. I feel like I'm trying to pay attention, but the exact details just somehow slip past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you understand the instructions or information correctly when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I understand things just fine. I mean, I know exactly what I'm supposed to do. If I read a set of instructions or look at the schedule, it all makes perfect sense to me. \n\nIt's just... when it comes time to actually execute it, that's when the mistake happens. Like with the wood, I know exactly how long it needs to be, I just end up marking it wrong anyway. So, no, I don't think I'm misunderstanding the information at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're processing or taking in the information correctly when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean... it feels like I'm taking it in just fine. I look at the numbers or the paper, and they register in my head okay. So I think I'm processing it right. \n\nBut... I don't know, maybe I'm not? It's hard to tell exactly what's happening in my brain at that exact second. I just know that I read it, I understand it, and then somewhere between my head and my hands, something just gets crossed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are happening because you're not focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is a focus thing, yeah. I mean, that's really what brings me in to see you. I just keep messing up these little things. Like, I'll be looking right at the schedule for the senior center, and I'll end up writing down the wrong time for a class. Or with the boards I mentioned... I'll measure it once, but I still cut it a hair short because I just sort of skipped over the small print on the tape measure. So, yeah, I guess I'm just not focusing enough on those tiny details when I'm in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're noticing all the details when you're performing a task?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I guess I'm not noticing all of them. Like I was just saying, that's really why I'm here. I keep messing up those little things. I'll be looking right at the senior center schedule and write down the wrong time for a class. Or with the woodworking, I'll measure a board, but I still cut it a hair short because I just... skip over the small print on the tape measure. So I guess I'm missing those little details when I'm actually doing the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're distracted by things around you when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At the senior center, if people are talking at the next table or... if my phone lights up, my mind just jumps over there. And then I lose my place in whatever I was filling out, or building, or whatever I was doing. So yeah, the things around me definitely pull me away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making these little errors in a variety of tasks, or is it mostly in one specific type of activity?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just one thing, I suppose. I mean, like I said, it happens with the woodworking out in the garage, and it happens with the scheduling stuff at the senior center. I don't really know if it happens in a whole lot of other areas, mostly because... well, since I stopped working, I don't have a ton of different activities going on anyway. But yeah, between my hobbies and the center, it's definitely not just one specific type of task. It just sort of happens with whatever I'm trying to get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're aware of your own thoughts when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess I'm not paying much attention to what I'm thinking about right at that second. My mind is just sort of... somewhere else. I couldn't tell you exactly what's going through my head when I write down the wrong time or make a bad cut. I just know I'm not thinking about the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to plan and organize tasks successfully before you start working on them?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I struggle with that quite a bit, actually. Like... if I have a day where I need to run a few errands, get the workshop ready for a project, and maybe go to a doctor's appointment, I have a really hard time figuring out what order to do them in. I just can't seem to get a plan straight in my head. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping to do something else, and then by the end of the day I'm just rushing around trying to finish it all. So, no, planning and organizing isn't something I do very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or daze when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say a fog, exactly. Or a daze. It's not like I'm out of it or confused. It's just... like I said before, my mind is just somewhere else. I'm just not focusing on what my hands are doing. So no, I don't feel foggy, just distracted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if you slowed down and took your time when performing tasks, you would still make these little errors?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't... I haven't really paid attention to whether how fast I'm going makes a difference. Maybe it would help if I took my time, but I just don't know for sure if that would actually stop the mistakes. I can't say I've really tested it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make fewer mistakes if someone was guiding you step by step through the tasks?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. I mean... since I'm not working anymore, I don't really have anybody standing over my shoulder walking me through things. It's mostly just me doing my own projects around the house or running my errands. So, I don't know if having someone guide me step by step would help with the mistakes or not. I just haven't been in a situation like that lately to find out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actively thinking about the details of the task when you're performing it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Like I was saying, my mind is usually off on something else entirely. I'm not really focused on the little details of what I'm doing right then. I'm just kind of going through the motions while I think about whatever else is on my mind. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm actively thinking about the details at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're missing these little details because you're not looking carefully enough at the information in front of you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that's a big part of it. I'm just not looking closely enough. It's actually kind of what brings me in... I just keep messing up these little things. Like, I'll be at the senior center and I'll write down the wrong time on a class sign-up sheet, or I'll be working on a project at the house, measure a board once, and still end up cutting it a hair short because I just skipped over the small print or the little marks on the tape measure. I'm just not paying close enough attention to what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make the same kinds of mistakes if you were doing something that required your full attention?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I mean... I can't really think of anything I do these days that demands that kind of absolute, full attention. Since I'm retired, most of what I do is pretty low-stakes. So, I don't really know if I'd still make those same mistakes or if I'd suddenly do better. I just haven't been in a situation like that recently to know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make these mistakes if you were doing a task that you find very interesting or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really noticed if enjoying the task makes a difference. I mean, I'd like to think I pay better attention when it's something I actually like doing, but... I just don't have a clear answer for you on that. I haven't really kept track of whether it happens less then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Well, I guess what brings me in is that I just keep messing up little things. It's nothing huge, but... well, for example, I'll write down the wrong time on a class sheet down at the senior center. Or I'll be working on a project, measure a board once, and still cut it a hair short because I somehow skipped over the small print on the tape measure. I just keep making these little detail mistakes, and it's getting frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're forgetting things more often than you used to?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. My memory seems about the same as it always has been. I mean, I'm not forgetting appointments or misplacing things any more than usual. It's really just... well, making those little errors when I'm actually doing something, rather than forgetting things completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're paying attention when you make these little errors?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I am. I mean, I feel like I'm looking right at the tape measure or the paper when I'm doing it. But... I don't know. Maybe my mind is just wandering for a split second and I don't realize it. It's hard to say for sure. I feel like I'm trying to pay attention, but the exact details just somehow slip past me anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you understand the instructions or information correctly when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I understand things just fine. I mean, I know exactly what I'm supposed to do. If I read a set of instructions or look at the schedule, it all makes perfect sense to me. \n\nIt's just... when it comes time to actually execute it, that's when the mistake happens. Like with the wood, I know exactly how long it needs to be, I just end up marking it wrong anyway. So, no, I don't think I'm misunderstanding the information at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're processing or taking in the information correctly when you're making these mistakes?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean... it feels like I'm taking it in just fine. I look at the numbers or the paper, and they register in my head okay. So I think I'm processing it right. \n\nBut... I don't know, maybe I'm not? It's hard to tell exactly what's happening in my brain at that exact second. I just know that I read it, I understand it, and then somewhere between my head and my hands, something just gets crossed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes are happening because you're not focusing on the task at hand?\n\nPatient: I suppose it is a focus thing, yeah. I mean, that's really what brings me in to see you. I just keep messing up these little things. Like, I'll be looking right at the schedule for the senior center, and I'll end up writing down the wrong time for a class. Or with the boards I mentioned... I'll measure it once, but I still cut it a hair short because I just sort of skipped over the small print on the tape measure. So, yeah, I guess I'm just not focusing enough on those tiny details when I'm in the middle of doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're noticing all the details when you're performing a task?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I guess I'm not noticing all of them. Like I was just saying, that's really why I'm here. I keep messing up those little things. I'll be looking right at the senior center schedule and write down the wrong time for a class. Or with the woodworking, I'll measure a board, but I still cut it a hair short because I just... skip over the small print on the tape measure. So I guess I'm missing those little details when I'm actually doing the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're distracted by things around you when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At the senior center, if people are talking at the next table or... if my phone lights up, my mind just jumps over there. And then I lose my place in whatever I was filling out, or building, or whatever I was doing. So yeah, the things around me definitely pull me away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself making these little errors in a variety of tasks, or is it mostly in one specific type of activity?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just one thing, I suppose. I mean, like I said, it happens with the woodworking out in the garage, and it happens with the scheduling stuff at the senior center. I don't really know if it happens in a whole lot of other areas, mostly because... well, since I stopped working, I don't have a ton of different activities going on anyway. But yeah, between my hobbies and the center, it's definitely not just one specific type of task. It just sort of happens with whatever I'm trying to get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're aware of your own thoughts when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I guess I'm not paying much attention to what I'm thinking about right at that second. My mind is just sort of... somewhere else. I couldn't tell you exactly what's going through my head when I write down the wrong time or make a bad cut. I just know I'm not thinking about the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to plan and organize tasks successfully before you start working on them?\n\nPatient: Well, not really. I struggle with that quite a bit, actually. Like... if I have a day where I need to run a few errands, get the workshop ready for a project, and maybe go to a doctor's appointment, I have a really hard time figuring out what order to do them in. I just can't seem to get a plan straight in my head. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping to do something else, and then by the end of the day I'm just rushing around trying to finish it all. So, no, planning and organizing isn't something I do very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or daze when you're making these little errors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say a fog, exactly. Or a daze. It's not like I'm out of it or confused. It's just... like I said before, my mind is just somewhere else. I'm just not focusing on what my hands are doing. So no, I don't feel foggy, just distracted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if you slowed down and took your time when performing tasks, you would still make these little errors?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't... I haven't really paid attention to whether how fast I'm going makes a difference. Maybe it would help if I took my time, but I just don't know for sure if that would actually stop the mistakes. I can't say I've really tested it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make fewer mistakes if someone was guiding you step by step through the tasks?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. I mean... since I'm not working anymore, I don't really have anybody standing over my shoulder walking me through things. It's mostly just me doing my own projects around the house or running my errands. So, I don't know if having someone guide me step by step would help with the mistakes or not. I just haven't been in a situation like that lately to find out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actively thinking about the details of the task when you're performing it?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Like I was saying, my mind is usually off on something else entirely. I'm not really focused on the little details of what I'm doing right then. I'm just kind of going through the motions while I think about whatever else is on my mind. I'm just not paying close enough attention to what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make the same kinds of mistakes if you were doing something that required your full attention?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I'm just not paying close enough attention to what's right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make the same kinds of mistakes if you were doing something that required your full attention?\n\nPatient: I'm honestly not sure. I mean... I can't really think of anything I do these days that demands that kind of absolute, full attention. Since I'm retired, most of what I do is pretty low-stakes. So, I don't really know if I'd still make those same mistakes or if I'd suddenly do better. I just haven't been in a situation like that recently to know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make these mistakes if you were doing a task that you find very interesting or enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that. I haven't really noticed if enjoying the task makes a difference. I mean, I'd like to think I pay better attention when it's something I actually like doing, but... I just don't have a clear answer for you on that. I haven't really kept track of whether it happens less then.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would make the same mistakes if someone else double-checked your work before you finished it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about that either.
